FILE: src\content\articles\how-do-scientific-explanations-go-wrong.md
================================================================
date: 2026-09-04
title: How Do Scientific Explanations Go Wrong?
slug: how-do-scientific-explanations-go-wrong
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-09-04
revised: 2026-09-05
cluster: methodology-and-explanation
role: core
abstract: >-
  Scientific explanations can go wrong in different ways. They may compare
  accounts that answer different questions, claim causation from insufficient
  evidence, overstate model validation, generalise beyond an established
  domain, conceal exploratory flexibility, substitute narrative coherence for
  evidential discrimination, or present integration as demonstrated
  explanatory advance. This article distinguishes these failures and shows why
  correction should be calibrated to the particular defect rather than treated
  as wholesale scientific rejection.
keyPoints:
  - Explanatory failure has distinguishable forms and should not be reduced to a single general verdict.
  - Explanations can be miscompared when they address different targets, contrasts or grains.
  - Association, plausibility and model fit do not by themselves establish stronger causal or representational claims.
  - Local evidence does not determine its own wider scope, and exploratory discovery should not be presented as independent confirmation.
  - A model, concept or framework may remain scientifically useful after an overextended explanatory claim is narrowed.
relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - biological-explanation
  - explanation
  - explanandum
  - explanatory-target
  - mechanism

FILE: src\content\articles\how-does-scientific-explanation-reach-reality.md
================================================================
date: 2026-08-09
title: "How Does Scientific Explanation Reach Reality?"
slug: how-does-scientific-explanation-reach-reality
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-08-09
revised: 2026-08-10

cluster: methodology-and-explanation
role: synthesis

abstract: >
  Scientific explanation is an epistemic achievement, but it is directed
  towards a reality that exists independently of any particular explanatory
  perspective. This article examines how explanatory practices can support
  ontological commitment without being treated as transparent representations
  of the world. It argues that explanation reaches reality through empirically
  constrained, perspectival, and revisable forms of understanding whose success
  provides defeasible grounds for ontological commitment. APS contributes to
  this problem by treating Agency, Process, and Scale as analytic projections
  rather than independent components of reality. Their integration does not
  reproduce the organisation of life without epistemic mediation, but
  progressively clarifies the viability-oriented, constraint-closed
  organisation that biological inquiry seeks to understand. APS therefore
  connects science's epistemological and ontological objectives without
  collapsing either into the other.

keyPoints:
  - Scientific explanation is epistemic in form but ontological in orientation.
  - Explanation does not reach reality through an unmediated correspondence between theory and world.
  - Empirical constraint, intervention, explanatory integration, and resistance to failure provide defeasible grounds for ontological commitment.
  - Perspectivism need not entail relativism because explanatory perspectives remain answerable to a reality they do not create.
  - Explanatory pluralism can disclose different aspects of one organised reality without requiring either reduction or unrestricted equivalence.
  - Agency, Process, and Scale are analytic projections of viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation, not independent components of reality.
  - Within APS, scientific explanation may contribute to metaphysics while remaining revisable, practice-constrained, and epistemically modest.

FILE: src\content\articles\human-values.md

title: Human Values
date: 2026-08-04
slug: human-values
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-08-04
revised: 2026-08-04
cluster: social-organisation
role: anchor

abstract: >
  Human values are fundamental to every form of collective life, yet their
  biological foundations and organisational role remain conceptually
  fragmented across biology, psychology, anthropology, sociology, and
  philosophy. Agency–Process–Scale (APS) interprets human values as stable
  patterns of significance organised through shared evaluation that guide the
  coordination of collective life. Rather than treating values as arbitrary
  preferences or timeless moral truths, APS explains them as organisationally
  real patterns emerging from biological evaluation and becoming stabilised
  through social organisation. This article establishes the canonical APS
  account of Human Values and provides the conceptual bridge between
  Biological Evaluation, Significance, Shared Evaluation, Social Norms,
  Morality, and Ethics.

keyPoints:
  - Human values are stable patterns of significance organised through shared evaluation.
  - Human values originate in biological evaluation through significance.
  - Values are organisationally real because they contribute to the coordination of collective life.
  - Human values are not reducible to preferences, beliefs, goals, or moral rules.
  - Values provide the conceptual bridge between biological organisation and social organisation.
  - Shared evaluation explains how values become socially organised.

FILE: src\content\articles\information-in-biology-an-aps-clarification.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-15
title: "Information in Biology: An APS Clarification"
slug: information-in-biology-an-aps-clarification
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
abstract: >
  Information is one of the most widely used and ambiguously defined concepts
  in biology. Genes are described as informational, nervous systems as
  information-processing systems, and organisms as informationally coupled to
  their environments. APS argues that biological information should not be
  treated as the foundational explanatory basis of life. Informational
  significance arises within viability-oriented living organisation in which
  differences can affect regulation, evaluation, coordination, and continued
  functioning. Biological information is therefore interpreted relationally
  and organisationally: what counts as information depends on how differences
  participate in the organised persistence of living systems. This article
  clarifies that claim while distinguishing biological significance from
  statistical information and locating information in relation to semiosis,
  meaning, representation, cognition, and biological agency.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\malfunction-breakdown-and-death-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-07
title: "Malfunction, Breakdown, and Death — Failure in Viability-Oriented Systems"
slug: malfunction-breakdown-and-death
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-07
revised: 2026-09-09
cluster: diagnostics-and-empirical-tractability

abstract: >
  APS treats malfunction, breakdown, and death as revealing relations to
  organised persistence within viability-oriented living systems. Malfunction
  is impaired functioning within an organisation that continues to persist;
  breakdown is erosion of the capacity through which that organisation
  maintains and re-establishes the conditions of its persistence; and death
  is collapse of the viability-oriented organisation through which the
  organism exists as that organism. Failure therefore provides a diagnostic
  window into biological organisation without replacing the mechanistic and
  empirical explanations required for particular cases.

keyPoints:
  - Viability-orientation does not imply indefinite persistence.
  - Biological vulnerability follows from the continuing activity required to sustain organised persistence.
  - Malfunction is impaired functioning within a living organisation that continues to persist.
  - Breakdown is erosion of the organisation's capacity to maintain and re-establish the conditions of its persistence.
  - Death is collapse of the viability-oriented organisation through which the organism persists as that organism.
  - Failure can reveal organisational dependencies that remain less visible during relatively stable functioning.
  - Ageing provides a comparative test case for distinguishing damage-based, programmatic, cybernetic, and organisational explanations of biological deterioration.
  - APS does not assume that organisational explanation provides greater explanatory adequacy than mechanistic or other competing accounts.

  - viability

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\meaning-in-biology-an-aps-clarification-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-15
title: "Meaning in Biology — An APS Clarification"
slug: meaning-in-biology-an-aps-clarification
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: clarification

abstract: >
  Meaning is often invoked in biology when organisms respond to signals,
  interpret environmental differences, regulate activity, communicate, or
  behave in ways sensitive to context. APS distinguishes these phenomena from
  biological significance itself. Biological evaluation generates significance:
  differences become relationally real for living systems because they bear
  differently upon viability. Significance is therefore not yet meaning.
  Integration and cognition can organise biological significance across time and
  across viability-relevant possibilities, while semiosis, information, and
  representation identify further relations that must not be conflated with one
  another. This article clarifies the biological conditions relevant to the
  emergence of meaning without attributing meaning wherever biological
  significance occurs.

FILE: src\content\articles\multiple-realization-and-biological-organisation.md

date: 2026-05-27
title: "Multiple Realization and Biological Organisation — An APS Clarification"
slug: multiple-realization-and-biological-organisation

abstract: >
  This article reinterprets the historical debate over multiple
  realization through the APS framework. APS argues that the debate
  emerged from attempts to understand how biological and cognitive
  organisation can remain explanatorily coherent across materially
  distinct implementations, but became unstable because it lacked an
  explicit account of viability-oriented organisation. APS therefore
  reconstructs realization in organisational rather than merely
  functional terms by grounding explanatory equivalence in
  persistence-maintaining, constraint-closed systems whose mechanisms
  participate within continuity-preserving biological organisation.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\naturalising-life-how-biological-concepts-become-scientifically-intelligible-ci.md
================================================================
title: Naturalising Life
slug: naturalising-life
description: >
  Naturalising Life examines an APS methodological approach to difficult
  biological concepts. It asks how naturalisation and conceptual closure can
  help move concepts such as purpose, agency, cognition, significance, meaning,
  and mind from conceptual ambiguity toward scientifically assessable
  formulations without treating conceptual coherence as empirical validation
  or explanatory superiority.
type: article
date: 2026-07-08
revised: 2026-09-02
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-08
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: synthesis

abstract: |
  Many important biological concepts—including purpose, agency, cognition,
  significance, meaning, and mind—remain conceptually contested despite
  substantial empirical investigation. This article examines an APS
  methodological proposal for addressing such difficulties through
  naturalisation and conceptual closure. Naturalisation seeks biologically
  tractable formulations of difficult phenomena, while conceptual closure
  clarifies the terms, relations, boundaries, and explanatory targets needed
  for those formulations to become scientifically assessable. Neither process
  establishes that a resulting formulation is biologically correct,
  explanatorily superior, or empirically validated. Their contribution is
  methodological: they can help transform conceptual ambiguity into explicit
  claims capable of independent empirical and comparative assessment. The
  article illustrates this approach through purpose, agency, cognition,
  significance, and meaning, and considers its possible role within the wider
  Matter-to-Mind research programme.

keyPoints:
  - Naturalisation can help formulate difficult biological concepts in scientifically tractable terms.
  - Conceptual closure concerns sufficient clarity of terms, relations, boundaries, and explanatory targets for assessment.
  - Naturalisation and conceptual closure do not by themselves establish biological truth, explanatory superiority, or empirical validation.
  - Biological agency is viability-oriented organisational activity.
  - Purpose concerns viability-oriented organisation without requiring intention.
  - Cognition, significance, meaning, mind, and related concepts retain proposition-specific evidential burdens.
  - APS uses naturalisation and conceptual closure as methodological resources rather than as a universal foundation for biological explanation.

relatedGlossaryItems:
  - naturalisation
  - conceptual closure
  - scientific intelligibility
  - biological agency

FILE: src\content\articles\normativity-in-biology-why-some-things-matter-to-living-systems.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-08
title: "Normativity in Biology — Why Some Things Matter to Living Systems"
slug: "normativity-in-biology-why-some-things-matter-to-living-systems"
type: article
abstract: >
  This article introduces biological normativity in APS as the intrinsic,
  viability-relative asymmetry through which living systems distinguish
  persistence-supporting from persistence-undermining conditions. Normativity
  emerges because living systems exist under conditions where organised
  continuity can succeed or fail. APS therefore explains normativity not as
  externally imposed value or subjective judgment, but as an organisational
  feature of viability-oriented persistence enacted through temporally
  organised biological activity. This article situates normativity within the
  broader framework of organised persistence explored further in *Function
  and Normativity — Why Biological Organisation Matters*.

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-28
revised: 2026-06-15
cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: core

keyPoints:
  - Biological normativity arises from viability-oriented organised persistence.
  - Living systems distinguish persistence-supporting from persistence-undermining conditions.
  - Normativity emerges because organised continuity can succeed or fail.
  - Normativity is organisational rather than subjective or externally imposed.
  - Vulnerability and the possibility of breakdown are central to biological normativity.
  - Cognition presupposes biological normativity rather than generating it.
  - APS naturalises normativity through organised persistence rather than external teleology.

FILE: src\content\articles\organism-environment-coupling-and-organised-persistence.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-28
title: "Organism–Environment Coupling — Distributed Persistence Across Living Systems"
slug: organism-environment-coupling
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-28
revised: 2026-09-09
cluster: ecological-organisation
role: core

abstract: >
  This article develops an APS account of organism–environment coupling as a
  materially realised relation through which living systems and environmental
  conditions can affect one another in ways relevant to organised persistence.
  APS rejects both the treatment of organisms as explanatorily self-contained
  and the stronger claim that organism and environment universally form a
  single coupled system. Organism–environment relations may contribute
  constitutively to particular biological explananda, but coupling does not
  itself generate agency, significance, cognition, continuity, or multiscale
  organisation. Its explanatory relevance depends on the biological target,
  the material relations involved, the difference those relations make, and
  the spatial and temporal extent required to explain them.

keyPoints:
  - Organism–environment coupling consists in materially realised relations between living systems and environmental conditions.
  - Some organism–environment relations can be constitutively relevant to conditions of organised persistence.
  - Coupling can support, reorganise, or undermine conditions relevant to viability; it is not intrinsically persistence-promoting.
  - Biological agency can modulate organism–environment relations, but coupling does not generate agency.
  - Organism–environment differences acquire biological significance through Biological Evaluation, not through coupling alone.
  - Coupling, ecological embeddedness, responsiveness, significance, or semiosis do not by themselves establish cognition.
  - Scale identifies the spatial and temporal extent required to explain materially relevant coupling relations; scales themselves do not couple.
  - The explanatory contribution of coupling must be established relative to a specified explanandum and comparator.

slug: organism-world-coupling-agency-not-control

abstract: |
  Organisms depend profoundly upon their environments, but dependence is not the same as control. This article explains why APS understands agency as the organisation of viable organism–world coupling rather than behaviour determined either from inside or outside.
keyPoints:
  - Organisms depend upon their environments, but environmental dependence does not imply environmental control.
  - Agency is enacted through viability-oriented organism–world coupling rather than isolated internal behaviour.
  - Coupling differs from control because living systems organise their environmental dependence from within their own viability constraints.
  - Perturbation distinguishes internally organised coupling from externally maintained control.
  - APS avoids both internalism and externalism by grounding biological normativity within the organism's own organisation.

FILE: src\content\articles\persistence-organised-continuity-through-time-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-22
title: "Persistence — Organised Continuity Through Time"
slug: persistence-organised-continuity-through-time
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-22
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: anchor

abstract: |
  Persistence is a central explanatory concept within APS because living systems remain continuous despite ongoing material, physiological, developmental, and environmental change. APS treats persistence not as static endurance or material permanence but as organised continuity through transformation. Life itself is defined as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation; organised persistence identifies the further explanatory problem of how that organisation maintains and re-establishes the conditions of its continuity through time. Agency, Process, and Scale provide complementary analytic projections through which this problem can be investigated. Organised persistence is therefore a principal APS explanatory target without being the universal explanandum of biology.

keyPoints:

  - Persistence in living systems concerns organised continuity through transformation rather than static endurance or material permanence.

  - Life is viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation; organised persistence is the explanatory problem of how that organisation maintains and re-establishes continuity through change.

  - Biological agency is the viability-oriented organisational activity through which living systems can maintain, modify, and re-establish conditions required for continued functioning.

  - Process concerns how continuity is maintained despite change, including through regulation, development, repair, resilience, and reorganisation.

  - Scale concerns where persistence and its relevant dependencies are organised across spatial and temporal extents.

  - Organisational continuity provides an important basis for investigating biological identity without reducing identity to material sameness.

  - Viability-oriented organisation establishes biological normativity through viability-relative asymmetries among conditions, processes, and outcomes.

  - Organised persistence can connect questions across biological domains where continuity is genuinely part of the explanandum, but it is not the obligatory target of biological explanation.

FILE: src\content\articles\physiology-and-evolution-in-aps-two-temporal-perspectives-on-the-same-organisation-ci.md

date: 2026-04-16
title: "Physiology and Evolution in APS — Two Temporal Perspectives on Organised Persistence"
slug: physiology-and-evolution-in-aps-two-temporal-perspectives-on-the-same-biological-organisation
type: article

abstract: >
  Physiology and evolution are often treated as separate explanatory domains:
  physiology explaining how organisms function in the present and evolution
  explaining how they change through time. APS argues that both address the
  same explanatory target—organised persistence—viewed across different
  temporal horizons. Physiology explains how organised persistence is enacted,
  regulated, and maintained within lifetimes, while evolution explains how
  lineages of organised persistence are reproduced, transformed, and
  historically diversified across generations. Understanding their
  relationship clarifies how biological continuity is maintained through
  continual transformation and reveals a deeper unity underlying biological
  explanation.

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-22
revised: 2026-06-22
cluster: evolutionary-dynamics
role: synthesis

keyPoints:
  - "Physiology and evolution address the same explanatory target: organised persistence."
  - Physiology explains how organised persistence is enacted and maintained in the present.
  - Evolution explains how lineages of organised persistence are transformed through historical time.
  - Development links physiological continuity and evolutionary continuity.
  - Adaptation connects present regulation with long-term evolutionary transformation.
  - Biological explanation requires integration across interacting temporal scales.

  Contemporary research has transformed our understanding of plants from
  passive organisms into active biological agents whose persistence depends
  upon continual regulation, signalling, development, and ecological
  organisation. Rather than treating these discoveries as independent
  explanatory domains, APS interprets them as complementary expressions of
  viability-oriented organised persistence. This article synthesises the
  contemporary literature on plant agency and shows how signalling,
  cognition, organism–environment coupling, ecological interaction, and
  distributed regulation become mutually intelligible within a single
  organisational framework centred on biological agency.

  - Agency does not arise from brains or nervous systems but from the viability-oriented organisation of living systems.
  - Signalling serves biological agency by coordinating the regulation required for organised persistence.
  - Plant agency reveals general organisational principles shared across all living systems rather than uniquely botanical phenomena.
  - APS integrates contemporary theories of plant agency within a unified explanatory framework centred on viability-oriented organised persistence.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\plant-cognition-aps-interpretation.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-04
title: "Plant Cognition — A Critical Review and APS Interpretation"
slug: plant-cognition-aps-interpretation
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-09-11
revised: 2026-09-12
cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: core

abstract: "Whether plants are cognitive cannot be decided either by the absence of nervous systems or by the presence of adaptive responsiveness, signalling, plasticity and learning-like activity. APS approaches plant cognition as a comparative explanatory problem. Plants clearly exhibit biological agency, biological evaluation and biological significance, but these pre-cognitive capacities do not by themselves establish cognition. APS defines cognition as the organisation of biological significance across time and currently proposes that cognitive attribution becomes warranted where integrated biological significance modulates activity across a temporal field of viability-relevant possibilities in ways not exhausted by immediate or fixed regulation. Applying this boundary to plant phenomena produces a differentiated result. Distributed sensing, systemic signalling and developmental plasticity establish sophisticated biological organisation but do not independently warrant cognition. Stress priming provides a stronger boundary case but remains cognitively indeterminate because temporally extended molecular, physiological and developmental regulation remains an adequate explanation of current evidence. Habituation-like phenomena are suggestive, while stronger associative-learning claims remain empirically contested. Plants therefore provide a demanding test of whether cognition can be extended beyond nervous systems without collapsing into biological regulation in general."

keyPoints:
  - "Plant biological agency, Biological Evaluation, Significance, signalling, plasticity and temporal retention do not individually or cumulatively establish cognition."
  - "APS defines cognition as the organisation of biological significance across time and treats the current cognitive-boundary proposal as provisional rather than as a second definition."
  - "Plant stress priming is a principal boundary case but remains cognitively indeterminate because sophisticated temporally extended regulation remains an adequate comparator."
  - "Habituation-like plant phenomena are suggestive, while stronger associative-learning claims remain empirically contested."
  - "Plant cognition should be adjudicated for specified phenomena relative to adequate non-cognitive comparators rather than attributed globally to plants as a taxonomic group."

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - viability
  - biological-agency
  - normativity
  - biological-evaluation
  - significance
  - integration
  - cognition

date: 2026-05-27
title: "Processual Individuality"
slug: processual-individuality
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-28

cluster: evolutionary-dynamics
role: extension

abstract: >
  In APS, biological individuality is understood as a dynamically maintained
  continuity of viability-oriented organisation rather than a fixed material
  substance or static structural entity. Organisms persist through regulated
  transformation, repair, ecological coupling, developmental integration,
  and continuity-preserving organisation across time. APS therefore
  interprets individuality as processual: not because organisms are vague or
  unreal, but because biological identity is constituted through organised
  persistence rather than immutable material sameness. This article
  introduces the APS processual account of individuality and situates it
  within the broader continuity architecture developed further in
  *Biological Individuality — Organised Persistence Through Time*.

FILE: src\content\articles\reductionism-in-biology-an-aps-clarification-cib.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-20
title: "Reductionism in Biology — An APS Clarification"
slug: reductionism-in-biology-an-aps-clarification
type: article
abstract: >
  APS distinguishes material constitution from explanatory sufficiency,
  clarifying how biology can remain fully grounded in physics and chemistry
  without assuming that every biological explanandum is exhausted by
  component-level description. The framework preserves mechanistic analysis
  while asking when materially realised organisational relations make an
  additional explanatory difference. Living organisation is viability-oriented
  and constraint-closed, but neither organisational description nor broader
  explanatory extent is automatically superior. Explanatory adequacy must be
  established relative to the explanandum and the relevant comparator.
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-26
revised: 2026-09-09
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
keyPoints:
  - APS distinguishes material constitution from explanatory sufficiency.
  - Living systems remain fully grounded in physical and chemical processes.
  - Mechanistic explanation may be sufficient for appropriately specified biological explananda.
  - Organisational relations become explanatorily relevant where they make a demonstrated difference to the target.
  - Explanatory priority is question-relative and does not establish ontological priority.
  - Perturbation can reveal organisational dependencies not evident under relatively stable conditions.
  - APS rejects both reductive mechanism and vague holism.
  - Scale identifies the spatial and temporal extent required for an explanation; it does not provide an alternative causal hierarchy.

FILE: src\content\articles\reframing-biology-how-aps-reorganises-core-concepts-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-16
title: Reframing Biology – How APS Reorganises Core Concepts
slug: reframing-biology-how-aps-reorganises-core-concepts
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-04-16
revised: 2026-07-22
cluster: methodology-and-explanation

abstract: >
  APS does not replace biology's central concepts. It reorganises them.
  By treating life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation
  and agency as the activity through which that organisation is enacted,
  APS provides an explanatory grammar that integrates life, function,
  evolution, development, ecology, cognition, and biological explanation
  within a common framework of organised persistence. This article
  explains how that reorganisation reshapes biology's conceptual
  architecture while preserving the empirical achievements of modern
  biological science.

keyPoints: 
- APS is a framework for biological explanation rather than a competing biological theory.
- Life is understood as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
- Agency is the present-tense activity through which that organisation is enacted and sustained.
- Organised persistence provides the integrative centre of biological explanation.
- Function, purpose, and normativity arise from the organisation of viability rather than from external design or subjective judgement.
- Development, evolution, and ecology become complementary organisational perspectives on organised persistence.
- Biological Evaluation generates significance, Integration organises significance, and Cognition is the organisation of biological significance across time.
- Architectural Dependency provides the explanatory relationships through which biology's foundational concepts become mutually intelligible.
- APS supplies an explanatory grammar and conceptual infrastructure that integrate biology's major concepts while preserving the empirical achievements and explanatory autonomy of established biological disciplines.

FILE: src\content\articles\representation-when-and-whether-it-arises-in-biological-systems.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-15
title: "Representation — When and Whether It Arises in Biological Systems"
slug: representation-when-and-whether-it-arises-in-biological-systems
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
abstract: >
  Representation is often invoked to explain cognition, prediction, memory,
  planning, and intelligent behaviour. APS does not treat representation as a
  prerequisite of life, biological agency, biological evaluation, biological
  significance, or cognition. Biological Evaluation generates significance,
  while cognition organises biological significance across time and across
  viability-relevant possibilities. Representation becomes a further
  explanatory hypothesis when some materially implemented organisation appears
  to stand in for, model, or otherwise organise activity relative to absent,
  possible, or counterfactual conditions. Such an attribution requires evidence
  beyond differential responsiveness, information processing, biological
  significance, memory, anticipation, or cognition alone. APS therefore asks
  when representational description identifies an additional dependency and
  improves explanation of a specified biological explanandum.

title: "Rethinking Development"
slug: rethinking-development

abstract: >
  In APS, development is understood not as the execution of a static
  biological program, but as the regulated transformation of
  viability-oriented organisation across time. Living systems preserve
  continuity through continual structural, functional, ecological, and
  behavioural change by maintaining organised persistence across
  dynamically transforming developmental processes.

FILE: src\content\articles\semiosis-how-differences-come-to-matter-in-living-systems-ci.md

date: 2026-05-06
title: "Semiosis — How Differences Come to Matter in Living Systems"
slug: semiosis-how-differences-come-to-matter-in-living-systems
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: core
abstract: >
  Semiosis concerns sign relations within living organisation. APS does not
  treat semiosis as the process through which biological significance first
  arises: Biological Evaluation is the process through which agency generates
  significance. Semiosis becomes a further explanatory hypothesis where a
  biologically significant difference functions as a sign in relation to
  another condition, object, possibility, or state of affairs. Such an
  attribution requires evidence beyond causal sensitivity, information-bearing
  correlation, or viability-relative significance alone. APS therefore
  distinguishes significance, semiosis, meaning, information, representation,
  and cognition while asking when sign-relational organisation identifies an
  additional materially implemented dependency that improves explanation of a
  specified biological explanandum.

FILE: src\content\articles\space-time-and-organisation-in-biology.md
================================================================
title: Space, Time, and Organisation in Biology — An APS Clarification
slug: space-time-and-organisation-in-biology
type: article
date: 2026-05-01
revised: 2026-08-03
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-01
cluster: conceptual-foundations
abstract: >
  This article clarifies the roles of space and time in biological explanation
  within the APS framework. It argues that space and time are not primary
  explanatory domains in biology but are organisational dimensions through which
  viability-oriented processes are structured and understood. By situating
  spatial configuration and temporal extension within the dynamics of
  organisation, the analysis shows how biological systems are best understood as
  processual, scale-dependent, and structured by constraints that sustain
  viability rather than by static spatial form or linear temporal sequence.

FILE: src\content\articles\symbolic-coordination.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-29
title: "Symbolic Coordination"
slug: symbolic-coordination
type: article

abstract: >
  Symbolic coordination is the use of shared symbols, meanings, classifications,
  narratives, and representational systems to stabilise and extend coordination
  among organisms across space and time. Within APS, symbols are understood not
  primarily as representations of reality but as organisational devices that
  enable coordinated activity beyond immediate interactions. Symbolic systems
  support the preservation of expectations, the transmission of practices, the
  accumulation of knowledge, and the emergence of culture, institutions, and
  technology. This article develops an APS account of symbolic coordination as
  a major extension of social continuity architecture.

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-06-15

cluster: social-organisation

keyPoints:
  - Symbols function as mechanisms of coordination.
  - Symbolic systems stabilise shared expectations and meanings.
  - Symbolic coordination extends communication beyond immediate interactions.
  - Symbols enable coordination across larger populations and longer timescales.
  - Culture depends upon the preservation and transmission of symbolic coordination.
  - Symbolic systems form part of the continuity architecture underlying institutions and technology.

FILE: src\content\articles\temporal-organisation-and-organised-persistence.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-15
title: "Temporal Organisation and Organised Persistence"
slug: temporal-organisation-and-organised-persistence
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-22
revised: 2026-06-15
cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: core

abstract: >
  This article develops the temporal architecture of the APS framework.
  APS rejects the treatment of living systems as static entities merely
  located within external chronological time. Living systems actively
  organise continuity across time through developmentally and temporally
  structured processes that continuously regenerate, repair, reorganise,
  and transform viable organisation across changing conditions and
  interacting timescales. Biological persistence therefore depends upon
  continuity through transformation rather than static endurance.
  Temporal organisation is thus constitutive of biological explanation
  itself rather than an external background against which life unfolds.

FILE: src\content\articles\the-cognitive-organisation-of-life.md
================================================================
date: 2026-06-01
title: "The Cognitive Organisation of Life"
slug: the-cognitive-organisation-of-life
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-09-11
revised: 2026-09-11
cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: core
abstract: >
  Cognition belongs within biological organisation without being identical
  with life or biological agency. APS defines cognition as the organisation
  of biological significance across time. This article examines what cognitive
  organisation contributes once cognitive attribution has been independently
  warranted. Biological Agency, Biological Evaluation, Significance and
  Integration provide pre-cognitive foundations, while cognition arises where
  significance is organised across time in ways that can modulate present
  activity relative to viability-relevant circumstances and possibilities
  beyond what immediate or fixed regulation alone explains. Cognitive
  organisation need not be restricted to nervous systems, but neither
  non-neural complexity nor adaptive regulation establishes cognition by
  itself. Semiosis, information and representation may contribute to
  particular cognitive phenomena without constituting universal stages of
  cognition. The article therefore situates cognition as a materially
  implemented form of living organisation that can contribute to organised
  persistence while remaining distinct from life, regulation, intelligence,
  consciousness and mind.
keyPoints:
  - "Cognition is the organisation of biological significance across time and is not identical with life, biological agency, regulation or adaptive responsiveness."
  - "Biological Agency, Biological Evaluation, Significance and Integration establish pre-cognitive conditions from which cognitive organisation may arise."
  - "Cognitive attribution requires explanatory warrant beyond immediate or fixed regulation; temporal extension, complexity or anticipation alone do not establish cognition."
  - "Cognitive organisation may be materially implemented without nervous systems, but non-neural organisation does not by itself warrant cognitive attribution."
  - "Semiosis, information and representation may serve explanatory roles in particular cognitive phenomena without constituting universal stages between life and cognition."
relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - viability
  - biological-agency
  - normativity
  - biological-evaluation
  - significance
  - integration
  - cognition

  In APS, life is fundamentally developmentally organised.

  objects, but as temporally organised, viability-oriented systems

  evolutionary scales. Development is therefore not secondary to life,
  but constitutive of biological persistence itself.

FILE: src\content\articles\the-ecological-organisation-of-life-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-06-01
title: "The Ecological Organisation of Life"
slug: the-ecological-organisation-of-life
type: article

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-01
revised: 2026-06-24

cluster: ecological-organisation
role: anchor

abstract: >
  Ecology is often presented as the study of interactions between
  organisms and their environments. APS accepts this insight while
  placing it within a broader account of viability-oriented organised
  persistence. Organisms do not merely exist within ecological
  environments; their continuity depends upon ongoing ecological
  organisation distributed across resources, niches, organism–
  environment coupling, ecological resilience, developmental
  conditions, evolutionary processes, and forms of ecological
  significance that shape agency and cognition. Ecology therefore
  becomes one of the major continuity architectures through which
  living systems sustain viability across changing conditions,
  scales, and timescales. This article presents the APS synthesis
  of ecological organisation and explains why ecology is
  indispensable to the persistence of life.

FILE: src\content\articles\the-evolutionary-organisation-of-life-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-06-01
title: "The Evolutionary Organisation of Life"
slug: the-evolutionary-organisation-of-life
type: article

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-01
revised: 2026-06-15

cluster: evolutionary-dynamics
role: synthesis

abstract: >
  This article serves as the principal synthesis article of the APS
  Evolutionary Dynamics cluster. Whereas Evolution as the Historical
  Transformation of Organised Persistence establishes the APS account of
  evolution itself, the present article examines how the major components
  of evolutionary organisation fit together within a unified explanatory
  architecture. APS interprets evolution as a continuity architecture
  through which viability-oriented organised persistence extends beyond
  individual lifetimes. Inheritance preserves continuity, variation
  generates possibility, selection shapes historical trajectories,
  adaptation stabilises viability, and innovation expands organisational
  possibilities. These processes are inseparable from development,
  ecology, cognition, and social organisation. Evolution therefore
  becomes not merely a theory of biological change but an account of how
  life remains historically continuous while continually transforming
  itself across generations.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\the-philosophical-foundations-of-aps.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-14
title: "The Philosophical Foundations of APS"
slug: the-philosophical-foundations-of-aps
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-14
revised: 2026-09-15
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: synthesis

abstract: >
  APS is a comparative explanatory methodology for biology with explicit
  philosophical commitments concerning life, organisation, biological reality,
  and explanation. This article examines those commitments without treating
  them as a prior philosophical system from which biological conclusions
  follow. It distinguishes the canonical APS account of life from organised
  persistence as an explanatory problem, treats Agency, Process, and Scale as
  analytic projections of one living organisation, and clarifies how
  philosophical claims about organisation and explanatory practice must remain
  answerable to specified explananda, materially implemented dependencies,
  empirical evidence, relevant comparators, and explicit limits. It also
  distinguishes these established foundations from the more exploratory APS
  research architecture extending from life toward cognition, Mind, Selfhood,
  Reflective Agency, and Meaning.

keyPoints:
  - APS is a comparative explanatory methodology for biology with philosophical commitments, not a philosophical system from which biology is derived.
  - Life is viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
  - Organised persistence concerns the continuity of living organisation through change; it is not a second definition of Life or the universal target of every biological explanation.
  - Agency, Process, and Scale are analytic projections of one living organisation rather than independent causes, components, dimensions, domains, or levels.
  - Explanatory claims are relative to specified explananda and relevant comparators.
  - Organisational or dependency claims require appropriate material and empirical warrant when presented as claims about biological reality.
  - Different explanatory approaches may answer different biological questions; plurality alone does not establish integration, complementarity, competition, or explanatory superiority.
  - The pathway from Life toward Meaning is a research architecture whose individual relations require independent specification and support.
  - APS's philosophical commitments remain open to comparative, empirical, and philosophical assessment.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - life

  - organised-persistence

FILE: src\content\articles\theoretical-biology-and-philosophy-of-biology-boundary-overlap-and-division-of-explanatory-labour.md
================================================================
date: 2026-08-17
title: "Theoretical Biology and Philosophy of Biology: Boundary, Overlap, and Division of Explanatory Labour"
slug: theoretical-biology-and-philosophy-of-biology-boundary-overlap-and-division-of-explanatory-labour
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-08-17
revised: 2026-08-17
cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: synthesis

abstract: >
  Theoretical biology and philosophy of biology substantially overlap in
  subject matter, concepts, methods, and explanatory concerns, making their
  boundary difficult to locate through disciplinary identity alone. This
  article develops a comparative account of their division of explanatory
  labour by classifying epistemic products rather than disciplines, authors,
  or publication venues. It evaluates six discriminating cases using intended
  product, evidential responsibility, documented biological consequence, and
  functional non-redundancy, while retaining uptake, co-production, and null
  contribution as explicit controls. The comparison distinguishes
  second-order analysis, clarificatory contribution, ambiguous boundary cases,
  direct theoretical contribution, co-produced direct contribution, and null
  direct contribution. The results support a functional and product-relative
  boundary: philosophical work can contribute directly to theoretical biology
  when it participates in a biologically answerable explanatory or theoretical
  product, assumes responsibility to relevant biological evidence, and has
  documented consequences for biological inquiry. The article does not claim
  novelty for philosophy-in-science or interdisciplinary co-production; its
  narrower contribution is a comparative architecture for discriminating among
  these different forms of explanatory labour.

keyPoints:
  - Theoretical biology and philosophy of biology substantially overlap in subject matter, concepts, methods, and explanatory concerns.
  - Their boundary is better treated as a functional division of explanatory labour among epistemic products than as a fixed disciplinary division.
  - Intended product, evidential responsibility, documented biological consequence, and functional non-redundancy provide the principal comparative discriminators.
  - Uptake, conceptual sophistication, or interdisciplinary collaboration do not by themselves establish direct theoretical-biological contribution.
  - The same comparative architecture permits second-order, clarificatory, ambiguous, direct, co-produced direct, and null direct-contribution outcomes.

relatedGlossaryTerms: []

date: 2026-06-26
title: Time and Biological Organisation — An APS Perspective
slug: time-and-biological-organisation
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-26
revised: 2026-07-01

cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: synthesis

abstract: |
  Living systems do not merely exist through time; they continually organise themselves through time in ways that preserve viability. This article develops an APS interpretation of biological temporality, arguing that continuity, organised persistence, and temporal organisation are fundamental requirements of living systems. Regulation, development, learning, adaptation, ecology, and evolution are interpreted as complementary temporal expressions of viability-oriented organised persistence. Rather than treating time as an external backdrop to biological activity, APS understands temporality as an intrinsic organisational dimension of life and a central component of biological explanation.

keyPoints:
  - Living systems organise themselves through time rather than merely existing within it.
  - Living systems maintain continuity through continual change rather than through permanence.
  - Organised persistence requires the continual maintenance of viable continuity.
  - Regulation, development, learning, adaptation, ecology, and evolution are complementary temporal expressions of organised persistence.
  - APS interprets temporality as an organisational dimension of viability rather than an external background to biological activity.

FILE: src\content\articles\viability-the-organising-principle-of-biological-pesistence.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-28
title: "Viability — The Organising Principle of Biological Persistence"
slug: viability-the-organising-principle-of-biological-persistence
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-28
revised: 2026-09-07
cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: core

abstract: >
  Viability occupies a central organising position within the APS framework.
  Living systems do not merely remain extant but continually maintain and
  re-establish conditions compatible with their continued functioning across
  changing circumstances. Within APS, viability identifies these system-relative
  conditions and the asymmetries they establish between activities that support,
  undermine, or restore continued functioning. Viability is therefore analytically
  related to biological agency, organised persistence, function, normativity,
  development, ecology, and cognition without constituting an intermediate stage
  through which one becomes another. This article develops viability as an aspect
  of viability-oriented, constraint-closed living organisation and examines its
  role in understanding how living systems maintain continuity through continual
  transformation.

keyPoints:
  - Viability is not mere survival but concerns the system-relative conditions required for continued functioning.
  - Viability belongs within one viability-oriented, constraint-closed living organisation rather than forming an independent component or stage.
  - Biological agency is viability-oriented organisational activity.
  - Organised persistence concerns the maintenance and re-establishment of living organisation through change.
  - Viability-relevant conditions are maintained and re-established through temporally organised activity and organism–world relations.
  - Agency, Process, and Scale provide complementary analytic perspectives on the same living organisation.
  - Viability-relative asymmetry provides the APS basis for biological normativity and significance.
  - Living systems persist through continual transformation rather than static stability.

title: What Are Biological Values?
slug: what-are-biological-values
type: article
abstract: >
  Biological values arise from the evaluative activities of living systems.
  Through organised persistence, organisms distinguish conditions according
  to their significance for viability, functioning, and adaptive persistence.
  Repeated evaluations stabilise into patterns of biological importance that
  guide regulation, behaviour, and goal-directed activity. Within the
  Agency–Process–Scale (APS) framework, biological values provide a bridge
  between meaning, evaluation, human values, and morality while avoiding
  both moral reductionism and the assumption that values originate only in
  human culture.

title: What Are Social Norms?
slug: what-are-social-norms

abstract: Social norms are collectively sustained expectations that organise behaviour within communities. They emerge through shared evaluation, guide social interaction, coordinate responsibilities, and help maintain collective forms of life. Understanding social norms reveals how shared values become organised expectations and provides the foundation for understanding moral evaluation, morality, and ethics.
keyPoints:
  - Social norms are collectively sustained expectations governing conduct within a community.
  - Norms emerge from shared evaluation rather than from individual preference alone.
  - Norms help organise coordination, responsibility, accountability, and social interaction.
  - Social norms are maintained through recognition, reinforcement, criticism, and institutional support.
  - Norms can change through disagreement, reflection, learning, and changing conditions.
  - Not all social norms are moral norms.
  - Social norms form the bridge between shared values and moral evaluation.

FILE: src\content\articles\what-counts-as-explanatory-gain.md
================================================================
date: 2026-09-04
title: "What Counts as Explanatory Gain?"
slug: what-counts-as-explanatory-gain
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-09-04
revised: 2026-09-05
cluster: methodology-and-explanation
role: core

abstract: >
  Explanatory gain is not established merely because an explanation is novel,
  different, broader, more integrated or more useful. It is a bounded conclusion
  about additional explanatory capacity relative to a sufficiently strong
  explanation of the same target. This article examines how such gain can be
  demonstrated, why no additional gain and unresolved assessment are legitimate
  outcomes, and why local explanatory gain does not establish framework-wide
  superiority.

keyPoints:
  - Explanatory gain is relative to a specified explanatory target and a sufficiently strong relevant comparator.
  - A target-relevant difference is necessary for positive gain but does not by itself establish additional explanatory capacity.
  - Prediction, integration, unification, novelty, formalisation and usefulness may contribute to explanation without automatically constituting explanatory gain.
  - Positive gain, no additional gain and unresolved assessment are all legitimate bounded outcomes.
  - Local explanatory gain does not by itself establish the overall superiority of a theory or framework.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - biological-explanation
  - explanation
  - explanandum
  - explanatory-target

  - what-makes-an-explanation-successful

FILE: src\content\articles\what-happens-when-aps-is-tested-against-its-strongest-comparators.md
================================================================
date: 2026-09-05
title: "What Happens When APS Is Tested Against Its Strongest Comparators?"
slug: what-happens-when-aps-is-tested-against-its-strongest-comparators
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-09-05
revised: 2026-09-06
cluster: methodology-and-explanation
role: extension
abstract: "A worked application of comparative explanatory methodology tests three APS propositions against their strongest relevant comparators. The results differ by proposition and target: minimal cognition retains a non-redundant boundary-classificatory role without demonstrated additional substantive biological gain; the APS function proposition encounters comparator advantage; and the APS analytic interface provides coherent redescription without demonstrated additional methodological gain. The comparison supports proposition-specific, comparator-relative assessment rather than a framework-wide verdict."
keyPoints:
  - "Scientific frameworks should be tested proposition by proposition against the strongest relevant comparator on a specified explanatory target."
  - "Minimal cognition in Caenorhabditis elegans yields complementarity: APS retains a boundary-classificatory role, but additional substantive biological explanatory gain is not demonstrated."
  - "Coronary-artery malfunction yields comparator advantage: contemporary organisational accounts already perform the relevant present-tense viability-relative discrimination."
  - "The APS interface applied to the KaiABC oscillator yields redescription with possible heuristic utility, not demonstrated additional methodological gain."
  - "The three local results do not warrant a framework-wide APS success or failure verdict or reopening of the prior scientific-status determination."

  - biological-explanation
  - explanandum

title: What Is Biological Evaluation?

date: 2026-07-02

abstract: >
  Biological evaluation is the process through which living systems distinguish
  conditions according to their significance for viability, functional
  integrity, and adaptive persistence. In APS, evaluation emerges directly from
  biological agency and provides the foundation for adaptive regulation,
  meaning, cognition, mind, selfhood, values, and morality. By explaining how
  significance arises within living systems, biological evaluation serves as a
  central bridge between biological organization and human experience.

keyPoints:
  - Biological evaluation is the process through which living systems distinguish conditions according to their significance for viability and adaptive persistence.
  - Evaluation emerges directly from biological agency and provides the basis for adaptive regulation.
  - Evaluation establishes biological significance, explaining why some conditions matter while others do not.
  - Meaning emerges from evaluated significance.
  - Cognition develops through the organization of biologically significant information.
  - Mind and selfhood emerge through increasingly integrated forms of evaluative cognition.
  - Human values and morality arise through reflective forms of evaluation.
  - Biological evaluation is the process through which agency generates significance.

date: 2026-06-25
title: What Is Biological Regulation?
slug: what-is-biological-regulation
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-25
revised: 2026-07-01

cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: clarification

abstract: |
  Biological regulation is one of the most pervasive concepts in biology, yet its meaning often varies across disciplines. In the Agency–Process–Scale (APS) framework, regulation is understood as the coordination of viability-oriented organisation under changing conditions. Living systems remain viable only by maintaining continuity despite continual internal and external change, and regulation is one of the principal organisational processes through which organised persistence is achieved. Rather than serving as biology's ultimate explanatory target, regulation helps explain how living systems preserve viable organisation across multiple scales and timescales.

keyPoints:
  - Biological regulation coordinates living organisation under changing conditions.
  - Regulation arises because living systems must preserve viable continuity despite continual change.
  - Regulation is broader than homeostasis and extends across multiple biological scales.
  - Regulation is not equivalent to external control or biological agency.
  - In APS, regulation contributes to the maintenance of organised persistence.
  - Understanding regulation clarifies how familiar biological concepts fit within a unified explanatory framework.

FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-biological-significance.md
================================================================
title: What Is Biological Significance?
slug: what-is-biological-significance
date: 2026-07-05
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-05
revised: 2026-07-06
cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: core

abstract: >
  Living systems do not inhabit neutral worlds. Some conditions support survival, functioning, and adaptive persistence, while others threaten or constrain them. Explaining why certain conditions matter is therefore a fundamental biological problem. Within the Agency–Process–Scale (APS) framework, biological significance refers to the relevance that conditions acquire for living systems through biological evaluation. Significance emerges when agents distinguish conditions according to their consequences for ongoing activity, organisation, and persistence. It is therefore not identical with information, function, meaning, or value, although it contributes to each. Biological significance occupies a central position within the APS architecture because it provides the bridge between biological agency and the later emergence of meaning, cognition, and mind. Understanding biological significance helps explain how living systems organise their interactions with the world around what matters and why increasingly complex forms of cognition and experience remain grounded in this fundamental biological reality.

keyPoints:
  - Biological significance explains how conditions come to matter for living systems.
  - Significance emerges through biological evaluation.
  - Significance is distinct from information because it concerns relevance rather than difference.
  - Meaning, cognition, and mind depend upon significance.
  - Significance provides a bridge between biological agency and cognition.

  - biological-agency
  - biological-evaluation
  - significance
  - meaning
  - cognition

  - selfhood
  - reflective-evaluation

date: 2026-07-12
title: What Is Ethics?
slug: what-is-ethics
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-12
revised: 2026-07-12
cluster: social-organisation
role: anchor
abstract: Ethics is the organised reflective investigation of morality through which communities examine, interpret, justify, criticise, and revise moral beliefs, practices, responsibilities, obligations, institutions, and forms of moral concern. It emerges from morality but is not identical with it. Understanding ethics explains how moral life remains open to reflection, disagreement, criticism, and revision, enabling communities to continually reassess the moral arrangements through which collective life is organised.
keyPoints:
  - Ethics emerges when morality itself becomes the object of reflective investigation.
  - Ethics is distinct from morality but depends upon morality for its subject matter.
  - Ethics examines obligations, responsibilities, legitimacy, institutions, and forms of moral concern.
  - Ethical disagreement is not a failure of ethics but one of the conditions that makes ethical inquiry necessary.
  - Ethics contributes to the criticism, interpretation, justification, and revision of moral life.
  - Ethics is not restricted to academic philosophy but is present wherever communities reflect upon moral questions.
  - Ethics helps ensure that morality remains open to examination and change.

FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-integration.md

title: What Is Integration?
slug: what-is-integration
date: 2026-07-07
type: article
revised: 2026-07-07
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-07
cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: core
abstract: >
  Living systems constantly encounter conditions that matter to their continued
  existence. Food, danger, opportunity, memory, social relationships, and
  environmental change can all be significant. Yet significance alone does not
  produce cognition, meaning, mind, or selfhood. What matters must also be
  organised into coherent patterns of relation. In APS, this process is called
  integration. Integration is the process by which significant relations are
  organised into coherent functional wholes capable of coordinated activity. It
  provides the bridge between significance and cognition and helps explain how
  increasingly complex forms of biological organisation give rise to meaning,
  mind, selfhood, and reflective agency.
keyPoints:
  - Integration is the process by which significant relations become organised into coherent functional wholes capable of coordinated activity.
  - Integration provides the bridge between significance and cognition within the APS framework.
  - Cognition depends on integrated significance rather than significance alone.
  - Integration occurs across biological scales, from cells and organisms to cognitive, social, and ecological systems.
  - Increasingly integrated forms of cognition contribute to the emergence of mind, selfhood, meaning, and reflective agency.

FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-it-about-life.md

date: 2026-06-29
title: What Is It About Life? — The Search for Life's Defining Principle
slug: what-is-it-about-life
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-29
revised: 2026-06-29

cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: synthesis

abstract: >
  Throughout the history of biology and philosophy, researchers have repeatedly attempted to identify the defining principle of life. Some have emphasised form, purpose, energy, metabolism, self-production, autonomy, organisation, or information. This article examines these major traditions as competing explanatory grammars—different ways of making life intelligible. By comparing the explanatory problems each tradition sought to solve, it traces a long intellectual search for the essence of life and situates APS within that ongoing conversation.

keyPoints:
  - The history of life theories can be understood as a history of competing explanatory grammars.
  - Different traditions have attempted to explain life through form, purpose, energy, metabolism, organisation, self-production, autonomy, and persistence.
  - Defining life requires distinguishing between definition, diagnosis, and evidence.
  - APS interprets earlier traditions as illuminating different aspects of living organisation.
  - Agency, process, and scale provide an explanatory grammar for understanding viability-oriented organised persistence.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-life-a-biological-question-revisited.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-27
title: What Is Life? — A Biological Question Revisited
slug: what-is-life-a-biological-question-revisited
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-28
revised: 2026-09-07
cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: anchor
abstract: >
  Life remains one of biology's most fundamental yet persistently contested
  questions. Despite extraordinary advances across the biological sciences,
  no single account of life has achieved broad conceptual agreement.
  Competing approaches variously emphasise metabolism, reproduction,
  evolution, information, autonomy, autopoiesis, or organisational closure,
  each illuminating important aspects of living systems while leaving
  significant explanatory questions unresolved. This article examines why
  the question remains open and argues that the continuing debate reflects
  uncertainty concerning the organisational target of biological
  explanation. Rather than asking only which properties distinguish living
  systems, APS investigates the kind of organisation those properties
  express. Within APS, Life is defined as viability-oriented,
  constraint-closed organisation, while organised persistence identifies
  the problem of how that living organisation maintains continuity through
  change.

keyPoints:
  - The question "What is life?" remains one of biology's central unresolved questions.
  - Different approaches often explain different aspects of living systems rather than the same biological question.
  - Trait-based definitions describe important characteristics of living systems but do not by themselves settle how those characteristics are organisationally related.
  - APS approaches Life as an organisational rather than primarily classificatory problem.
  - Life is defined within APS as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
  - Organised persistence concerns how living organisation maintains continuity through change.
  - APS provides a comparative explanatory methodology for relating complementary biological perspectives without replacing established explanations.

relatedGlossaryTerms:

  - viability
  - organised-persistence
  - persistence
  - normativity

  - evaluation

title: What Is Meaning?
slug: meaning
date: 2026-07-08
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-08
revised: 2026-07-08
cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: core

abstract: Meaning is the interpretive organisation of significance within broader patterns of relation, consequence, value, purpose, identity, and understanding. Within APS, meaning emerges from the progressive organisation of significance through integration, cognition, mind, selfhood, and reflective agency. Meaning explains how what matters becomes understood, interpreted, and situated within larger frameworks of understanding. It therefore functions as the interpretive culmination of the Cognition–Mind pathway and the current endpoint of the Matter-to-Mind programme.

keyPoints:
  - Meaning is the interpretive organisation of significance.
  - Significance explains what matters; meaning explains what what matters means.
  - Meaning cannot be reduced to information, purpose, value, or reflective agency.
  - Meaning emerges in progressively richer forms as biological organisation becomes more sophisticated.
  - Meaning represents the interpretive culmination of the APS pathway.

  Mind is the integrated organisation of cognition and significance through which an agent maintains a coherent relationship with itself and its world. Within APS, mind emerges from the integration of cognition and serves as the organisational bridge between cognition and selfhood.

keyPoints:

  - Mind emerges from integrated evaluative cognitive processes.
  - Mind is distinct from both cognition and consciousness.
  - Mind integrates organised significance into coherent agent–world relations.
  - Selfhood develops from the stabilisation of minded organisation through time.

FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-morality.md

date: 2026-07-12
title: What Is Morality?
slug: what-is-morality
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-12
revised: 2026-07-12
cluster: social-organisation
role: anchor
abstract: Morality is the organised system of evaluative practices, responsibilities, obligations, justifications, and forms of concern through which communities regulate conduct in relation to morally considerable beings and forms of life. It emerges from moral evaluation but is not reducible to social norms, social consensus, institutional authority, biological persistence, or individual preference. Understanding morality explains how communities organise collective life around questions of legitimacy, responsibility, obligation, and moral concern.
keyPoints:
  - Morality emerges from moral evaluation but is not identical with moral evaluation.
  - Morality organises collective life around questions of obligation, responsibility, legitimacy, and moral concern.
  - Morality depends upon social norms but is not reducible to social norms.
  - Morality cannot be reduced to social consensus, institutional authority, or cultural continuity.
  - Moral disagreement does not eliminate morality but often reflects its continuing activity.
  - Morality concerns the treatment of morally considerable beings and forms of life.
  - Ethics emerges when morality itself becomes the object of reflective investigation.

FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-reflective-agency.md
================================================================
title: What Is Reflective Agency?
slug: what-is-reflective-agency
date: 2026-07-06
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-06
revised: 2026-07-06

cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: synthesis

abstract: >
  Reflective agency is the capacity of a persistent self to evaluate,
  direct, and transform its own agency. While agency enables living
  systems to act, cognition enables evaluation, mind enables coherent
  self–world organisation, and selfhood enables continuity through
  time, reflective agency introduces the possibility of self-directed
  transformation. Through reflective agency, agents become capable of
  examining and reshaping their goals, commitments, values, and modes
  of action. APSI therefore treats reflective agency as a distinct
  organisational achievement emerging from agency, cognition, mind,
  and selfhood. Reflective agency occupies a pivotal position between
  selfhood and meaning and provides the foundation for responsibility,
  deliberate self-change, value formation, and reflective forms of
  meaning.

keyPoints:
  - Reflective agency is the capacity of a persistent self to evaluate, direct, and transform its own agency.
  - Reflective agency emerges from agency, cognition, mind, and selfhood but is not reducible to any of them.
  - Selfhood explains persistence; reflective agency explains self-directed persistence.
  - Reflective agency is distinct from consciousness, self-awareness, metacognition, and autonomy.
  - Reflective agency provides the bridge between selfhood and meaning.
  - Through reflective agency, living systems become active participants in shaping their own future organisation.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - biological-agency
  - significance
  - cognition
  - mind

  - consciousness

FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-selfhood.md
================================================================
title: What Is Selfhood?
slug: what-is-selfhood
date: 2026-07-06
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-06
revised: 2026-07-06
cluster: cognition-and-mind
role: core

abstract: >
  Selfhood is often associated with consciousness, self-awareness, identity, or the human experience of being a self. Within APS, however, selfhood addresses a more fundamental biological question: how living systems remain themselves despite continual change. Organisms replace components, develop, learn, adapt, and reorganise while maintaining continuity across time. APS interprets this continuity not as the persistence of matter, essence, or identity, but as persistent integrative organisation. Selfhood therefore explains how living systems maintain continuity of agency, significance, cognition, and mind despite ongoing material, developmental, and experiential change. It occupies a pivotal position between mind and reflective agency within the APS architecture.

keyPoints:
  - Selfhood is the persistent integrative organisation through which living systems remain themselves despite continual change.
  - Selfhood is not identical with consciousness, self-awareness, identity, or personality.
  - Identity describes continuity, whereas selfhood explains how continuity is maintained.
  - Mind establishes coherent self–world organisation; selfhood maintains that organisation through time.
  - Selfhood provides the organisational bridge between mind and reflective agency.
