FILE: src\content\articles\what-is-the-meaning-of-life.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-01
title: What Is the Meaning of Life?
slug: what-is-the-meaning-of-life
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-02
revised: 2026-07-02

cluster: conceptual-foundations
role: synthesis

abstract: |
  The question of life's meaning has traditionally been addressed through
  religious, philosophical, and existential perspectives. In the
  Agency–Process–Scale (APS) framework, meaning is understood as a
  natural feature of living systems rather than an exclusively human
  concern. Meaning arises wherever conditions, relationships, events, or
  actions make a difference to the viability, persistence, flourishing,
  or continuity of living systems. This perspective distinguishes
  meaning from purpose, function, and goals while explaining how each
  emerges from biological organisation. Rather than locating meaning
  outside life, APS interprets meaning as an expression of the organised,
  agency-driven processes through which living systems maintain
  continuity across changing conditions. Human meaning represents a
  particularly reflective and symbolic manifestation of a more general
  biological phenomenon.

keyPoints:
  - Meaning concerns significance rather than purpose alone.
  - Meaning emerges wherever something matters to the persistence or flourishing of a living system.
  - Biological meaning is not restricted to humans.
  - Meaning, purpose, function, and goals are related but distinct concepts.
  - APS naturalises meaning through biological agency and organised persistence.
  - Human meaning represents a highly elaborated form of a broader biological phenomenon.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - meaning
  - biological-agency
  - organised-persistence
  - viability
  - purpose

FILE: src\content\articles\which-explanatory-framework-is-best.md
================================================================
date: 2026-09-05
title: Which Explanatory Framework Is Best?
slug: which-explanatory-framework-is-best
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-09-05
revised: 2026-09-05
cluster: methodology-and-explanation
role: core
abstract: Biological explanatory frameworks cannot be ranked globally without first specifying what is being explained and what the relevant alternatives are. This article shows how controlled, target-relative comparison can support bounded explanatory gain, complementarity, no additional gain, unresolved assessment or non-comparability without turning local results into a universal league table.
keyPoints:
  - "Frameworks should be compared for specified explanatory targets, not ranked globally."
  - "A local explanatory advantage does not establish framework-wide superiority."
  - "Explanatory difference, usefulness, integration or formal sophistication does not by itself establish explanatory gain."
  - "Controlled comparison can support bounded positive gain, complementarity, no additional gain, unresolved assessment or non-comparability."
  - "The strongest relevant comparator must receive full explanatory credit before additional explanatory capacity is claimed."

FILE: src\content\articles\why-AI-is-not-biological-agency-cia.md

date: 2026-05-14
title: "Why AI Is Not Biological Agency"
slug: why-ai-is-not-biological-agency
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-08-21
cluster: artificial-systems-and-boundaries
role: extension

abstract: >
  Contemporary artificial intelligence systems increasingly display adaptive,
  responsive, and apparently agent-like behaviour. These developments have
  encouraged claims that biological and artificial systems differ only in
  degree rather than kind. APS rejects this conclusion. Behavioural
  sophistication, optimisation, intelligence-like behaviour, and adaptive
  responsiveness alone are insufficient for biological agency. Biological
  agency depends upon viability-oriented, continuity-preserving,
  self-maintaining organisation sustained across time, whereas contemporary AI
  systems remain externally maintained optimisation systems lacking endogenous
  persistence, operational normativity, and viability-oriented organised
  self-maintenance.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\why-aps-is-not-hierarchical-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-09

title: Why APS Is Not Hierarchical

slug: why-aps-is-not-hierarchical

type: article

status: canonical

canonical: true

canonicalLockDate: 2026-04-09

revised: 2026-09-09

cluster: conceptual-foundations

role: clarification

abstract: >
  Biology is often described through hierarchical levels extending from
  molecules to ecosystems. Such representations can be useful for investigation,
  modelling, communication, and teaching, and some can track materially real
  part–whole or nested relations. Neither fact establishes that living
  organisation forms a universal hierarchy of discrete causal or explanatory
  levels. APS therefore does not replace hierarchy with interacting scales.
  It asks which materially realised processes, constraints, interactions,
  structures, and organisational relations are relevant to the explanandum.
  Scale then identifies the spatial and temporal extent across which the
  persistence relevant to that explanandum is organised. APS thus replaces the
  presumption of hierarchical ontology with analysis of organisation, while
  retaining hierarchical descriptions where they serve a legitimate
  explanatory purpose.

keyPoints:
  - Hierarchical descriptions can be useful without constituting a universal ontology of biological organisation.
  - Biology contains materially real part–whole, nested, and organisational relations; APS does not deny them.
  - APS does not replace hierarchical levels with interacting scales.
  - Materially realised processes, constraints, interactions, structures, and organisational relations perform biological causal work.
  - Scale identifies the spatial and temporal extent across which persistence relevant to an explanandum is organised.
  - Terms such as top-down and bottom-up causation should be resolved into the material relations they abbreviate.
  - Biological agency is viability-oriented organisational activity, not a property located at a privileged hierarchical level.
  - Explanatory domains need not correspond to ontological levels.
  - The spatial and temporal extent required for explanation is target-dependent.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - scale
  - biological-organisation
  - biological-agency
  - constraint-closure
  - normativity
  - process
  - viability

FILE: src\content\articles\why-aps-is-not-holism-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-28
title: "Why APS Is Not Holism"
slug: why-aps-is-not-holism
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-28
revised: 2026-08-10
cluster: philosophy-of-biology

abstract: >
  APS rejects both reductionism and holism as complete accounts of
  biological explanation. While holism correctly recognises that living
  systems exhibit organisation that cannot be understood through isolated
  components alone, APS argues that appeals to wholes, totalities, or
  organismic unity are themselves insufficient explanations. Biological
  organisation is neither reducible to parts nor explained by abstract
  wholes. Instead, APS explains living systems through viability-oriented
  organised persistence, constraint-closure, processual individuality,
  and continuity-preserving organisation across time. Holistic properties
  emerge from organisation, but organisation itself remains the primary
  explanatory target.

FILE: src\content\articles\why-aps.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-28
title: Why APS? Why Biology Still Needs an Organisational Framework for Life
slug: why-aps
type: orientation
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-28
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: anchor
abstract: >
  Modern biology has achieved extraordinary explanatory success through advances
  in evolution, genetics, molecular biology, physiology, developmental biology,
  ecology, and systems biology. Yet this success has also sharpened enduring
  questions concerning the nature of life, biological organisation, and the
  explanatory target of biology itself. Rather than proposing a new biological
  theory, the Agency–Process–Scale (APS) framework addresses these questions by
  developing an organisational research programme centred on
  viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation. This article explains the
  scientific and conceptual circumstances from which APS emerged, introduces
  its central organisational perspective, and asks what conceptual,
  methodological, or biological explanatory contribution that perspective can
  make alongside established approaches in theoretical biology.

keyPoints:
  - Modern biology has achieved extraordinary explanatory success while continuing to generate foundational questions about life.
  - Scientific progress reveals deeper conceptual questions as well as new empirical discoveries.
  - The remaining challenge in theoretical biology includes questions concerning the organisational targets of biological explanation rather than a lack of biological evidence.
  - APS was developed as an organisational research programme that complements rather than replaces established biological sciences.
  - APS investigates living systems as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation through the complementary analytic projections of Agency, Process, and Scale.
  - APS investigates whether an organisational perspective can clarify biological explananda, relate established explanations without conflating them, generate assessable questions, or provide additional explanatory gain where this is demonstrated.
  - This article serves as the principal orientation to the APS corpus and explains why the framework was developed.

date: 2026-05-17
title: "Why Cognition Cannot Be Separated from Organised Persistence"
slug: why-cognition-cannot-be-separated-from-organised-persistence
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-17
revised: 2026-07-28
cluster: cognition-and-mind

abstract: >
  Cognition is often treated as an abstract informational or computational
  process separable from the biological organisation of living systems. APS
  rejects this separation. This article argues that cognition emerges within
  viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and cannot be understood
  independently of the persistence dynamics through which living systems
  sustain themselves across time. Cognition is therefore approached not as an
  isolated mental or informational capacity, but as an increasingly integrated
  form of evaluation, semiosis, regulation, and adaptive organisation grounded
  in the evolutionary transformation of organised persistence.

FILE: src\content\articles\why-life-is-not-a-machine-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-28
title: "Why Life Is Not a Machine — An APS Clarification"
slug: why-life-is-not-a-machine
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-08-21
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: clarification

abstract: >
  Machine analogies and mechanistic explanations have played powerful roles
  throughout biology, illuminating causal organisation, functional relations,
  regulation, development, and interactions among biological components.
  APS nevertheless rejects the stronger identity claim that life is adequately
  defined as a machine. It defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed
  organisation and treats organised persistence as the explanatory problem of
  how that organisation maintains and re-establishes continuity through change.
  This article distinguishes the machine/non-machine question from the scientific
  adequacy of mechanistic explanation. It does not assume that mechanistic
  approaches are reductive, merely local, or explanatorily subordinate to APS.
  Where mechanistic and APS explanations address the same biological target,
  comparative gain requires target-matched assessment.

keyPoints:
  - Machine analogies and mechanistic explanations are not the same thing.
  - APS does not define life as a machine.
  - Mechanistic approaches provide substantive explanations across biological science and may be sufficient for particular explananda.
  - APS defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
  - Organised persistence concerns how living organisation maintains and re-establishes continuity through change.
  - Biological agency is viability-oriented organisational activity.
  - Rejecting machine identity does not establish the explanatory limits of mechanism.
  - Where APS and mechanistic explanations address the same target, comparative gain requires target-matched assessment.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\articles\why-life-is-not-active-inference-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-29
title: "Why Life Is Not Active Inference"
slug: why-life-is-not-active-inference
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology

abstract: >
  Active Inference and the Free Energy Principle provide influential frameworks for explaining adaptive regulation, prediction, learning, behavioural coordination, uncertainty management, and organism–environment relations. APS rejects the stronger identity claim that inference, prediction, optimisation, or free-energy minimisation by themselves define life. APS instead defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and treats organised persistence as the problem of biological continuity through change. This article clarifies the resulting difference without assuming that active inference merely describes biological activity while APS alone explains its constitutive organisation. Where the frameworks address the same explanatory target, comparative gain requires target-matched assessment.

keyPoints:
  - Active inference provides substantive explanatory and formal resources for adaptive regulation, learning, perception, action, and organism–environment coordination.
  - APS does not identify life with inference, prediction, optimisation, or free-energy minimisation.
  - APS defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and treats organised persistence as the problem of continuity through change.
  - Differences between inferential, formal, organisational, and biological descriptions do not by themselves establish explanatory superiority.
  - Relations between viability, evaluation, normativity, prediction, and inference may be investigated as explanatory or dependency hypotheses rather than assumed from APS architecture.
  - Active inference may overlap with, complement, differ from, or outperform APS for particular explanatory targets.
  - Comparative preference requires target-matched assessment.

FILE: src\content\articles\why-life-is-not-autopoiesis.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-27
title: Why Life Is Not Autopoiesis
slug: why-life-is-not-autopoiesis
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-04-27
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: clarification

abstract: >
  Autopoiesis transformed theoretical biology by making the self-producing
  organisation of living systems an explicit explanatory target. Later
  organisational and autonomy approaches developed related accounts of
  self-maintenance, closure, regulation, normativity, and biological agency.
  APS shares this concern with living organisation but does not identify life
  with autopoiesis. It defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed
  organisation and treats organised persistence as the explanatory problem of
  how that organisation maintains and re-establishes continuity through
  change. These differences establish non-identity and distinct explanatory
  architectures; they do not by themselves show that APS explains living
  organisation better than autopoietic or autonomy-based approaches.

keyPoints:
  - Autopoiesis made the self-producing organisation of living systems a major explanatory target in theoretical biology.
  - Autopoietic and later autonomy approaches provide substantive accounts of organisation, self-production, closure, self-maintenance, autonomy, normativity, and biological agency.
  - APS shares important organisational concerns with these approaches but is not identical with autopoiesis or autonomy theory.
  - APS defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and biological agency as viability-oriented organisational activity.
  - Organised persistence concerns how living organisation maintains and re-establishes continuity through change; it is not a second definition of life.
  - Differences among autopoietic, autonomy, and APS architectures do not establish a progression of explanatory superiority.
  - Where the approaches address the same explanatory target, comparative explanatory gain requires target-matched assessment.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - autopoiesis
  - constraint-closure
  - biological-agency
  - viability
  - normativity
  - evolution

abstract: >
  Computational approaches provide powerful explanatory and modelling resources
  throughout contemporary biology. They can illuminate regulation, learning,
  signalling, coordination, pattern formation, and other biological processes.
  APS nevertheless does not identify life with computation or computational
  organisation. It defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed

  - Organised persistence concerns how living organisation maintains and re-establishes continuity through change; it is not a second definition of life.
  - Artificial computation shows only that computational capacity alone is not sufficient for life as defined within APS.
  - Non-identity between APS and computational approaches does not establish explanatory superiority.
  - Where APS and computational explanations address the same target, comparative explanatory gain requires target-matched assessment.

date: 2026-05-15
title: Why Life Is Not Control Theory
slug: why-life-is-not-control-theory
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-15
revised: 2026-08-22
cluster: philosophy-of-biology

abstract: >
  Control theory provides powerful explanatory and mathematical resources
  for understanding regulation, feedback, stability, coordination, and
  adaptive dynamics in biological systems. These approaches range from
  engineering models of externally specified control to biological and
  cybernetic accounts of endogenous, distributed, and adaptive regulation.
  APS does not identify life with control architecture or regulation alone.
  It proposes instead that life is viability-oriented, constraint-closed
  organisation. This difference establishes a substantive point of comparison,
  but not explanatory superiority: where APS and control-theoretic approaches
  address the same biological target, their relative explanatory contribution
  requires target-matched assessment.

keyPoints:
  - Control theory provides substantive explanations of regulation, feedback, stability, coordination, and adaptive dynamics in biological systems.
  - Control-theoretic approaches are heterogeneous and need not depend on externally specified setpoints, centralised command, or static equilibrium.
  - APS does not identify life with control architecture or regulatory capacity alone.
  - Life is viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
  - Biological Evaluation and control are distinct concepts, but APS does not assume a universal dependency from one to the other.
  - Control may occur in artificial systems, showing only that control alone is not sufficient for life as defined within APS.
  - Non-identity between APS and control theory does not establish explanatory superiority.
  - Where APS and control-theoretic approaches address the same biological target, comparative explanatory gain requires target-matched assessment.

FILE: src\content\articles\why-life-is-not-enactivism-cic.md

date: 2026-04-27
title: Why Life Is Not Enactivism
slug: why-life-is-not-enactivism
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-04-27
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: clarification

abstract: >
  Enactivism approaches cognition through embodiment, action, sense-making,
  and dynamic organism–environment relations, with important variants
  extending these concerns into biological autonomy and the continuity
  between life and mind. APS shares several of these explanatory concerns
  but does not identify life with cognition, sense-making, or enactive
  organisation. It defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed
  organisation and biological agency as viability-oriented organisational
  activity, while treating cognition as requiring further organisational
  capacities. These differences establish a distinction in conceptual and
  explanatory architecture, not the explanatory superiority of APS over
  enactivism. Where the approaches address the same biological explanandum,
  their relative explanatory contribution requires comparison.

keyPoints:
  - Enactivism is a family of approaches emphasising embodiment, action, sense-making, and organism–environment relations.
  - Enactivist approaches make substantive contributions to explanations of cognition, autonomy, adaptivity, normativity, and the continuity between life and mind.
  - APS shares important concerns with enactivism but does not identify life with cognition, sense-making, or organism–environment coupling.
  - APS defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and biological agency as viability-oriented organisational activity.
  - APS distinguishes biological agency from cognition without treating that distinction as evidence that enactivist accounts are explanatorily inferior.
  - Differences between APS and enactivism establish non-identity and different explanatory architectures; comparative explanatory gain requires target-matched assessment.

  Information-processing approaches provide important explanatory resources
  across molecular biology, neuroscience, systems biology, cognitive science,
  cybernetics, and related fields. APS rejects the stronger identity claim
  that information processing by itself defines life. APS instead defines
  life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and treats
  organised persistence as the problem of how that organisation maintains
  and re-establishes continuity through change. This article distinguishes
  informational explanation from the APS account of living organisation
  without assuming that informational approaches are merely descriptive,
  derivative, or explanatorily subordinate. Where the approaches address the
  same explanatory target, comparative gain requires target-matched assessment.

keyPoints:

  - APS does not define life as information processing.
  - APS defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and treats organised persistence as the problem of continuity through change.
  - Information, biological significance, semiosis, representation, cognition, and meaning are distinct concepts within APS.
  - APS may investigate relations among viability, evaluation, significance, and information without treating their conceptual ordering as an established dependency.
  - Informational explanations may be sufficient or preferable for particular biological targets.
  - Comparative explanatory gain requires target-matched assessment rather than inference from conceptual architecture.

FILE: src\content\articles\why-life-is-not-intelligence-an-aps-clarification-ci.md

date: 2026-05-07
title: "Why Life Is Not Intelligence, An APS Clarification"
slug: why-life-is-not-intelligence-an-aps-clarification
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-29
revised: 2026-09-14
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: clarification

abstract: >
  Intelligence describes specialised capacities such as flexible problem-solving,
  abstraction, learning, planning, and adaptive coordination, but such capacities
  neither define nor by themselves establish life. APS defines life as
  viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and distinguishes biological
  agency from cognition and intelligence. Biological intelligence occurs within
  living organisation, while intelligence-like capacities may also be realised
  in non-living artificial systems. The resulting non-identity does not establish
  that APS is explanatorily superior to theories of intelligence, cognition, or
  artificial systems.

keyPoints:
  - Intelligence is not the APS definition of life.
  - Intelligence is neither required by the APS definition of life nor sufficient by itself to establish biological status.
  - Biological agency is viability-oriented organisational activity.
  - Cognition and intelligence are distinct explanatory concepts within APS.
  - Cognition is the organisation of biological significance across time.
  - Biological intelligence occurs within living organisation, but intelligence-like capacities need not imply life.
  - Constraint closure belongs to the APS definition of life; it does not by itself demonstrate explanatory superiority over theories of intelligence.
  - Comparative explanatory gain requires a specified shared explanandum and target-matched assessment.

  APS rejects the idea that organisms are independently constituted entities

  be distinguished physically from their surroundings, their viability depends

FILE: src\content\articles\why-philosophy-of-biology-matters.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-17
title: "Why Philosophy of Biology Matters"
slug: why-philosophy-of-biology-matters
type: article
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-17
revised: 2026-08-24
cluster: philosophy-of-biology
role: clarification

abstract: >
  Philosophy of biology examines the conceptual foundations, explanatory
  structures, and ontological assumptions underlying biological science.
  Biology repeatedly generates philosophical problems because living systems
  maintain viability-oriented organisation through continual material and
  structural change. This organised persistence raises questions concerning
  function, agency, normativity, teleology, individuality, cognition, and
  meaning that cannot be settled by empirical description alone. This article
  explains why such questions continually reappear within biological
  explanation and situates APS as a comparative explanatory methodology within
  the broader landscape of contemporary philosophy of biology and theoretical
  biology.

keyPoints:
  - Biology generates philosophical problems because living systems exhibit organised persistence.
  - Biological explanation repeatedly invokes organisation, normativity, function, and purposiveness.
  - Whether organisational analysis adds to mechanistic or other established explanations must be assessed comparatively.
  - Teleology and teleonomy reappear because organisms regulate activity relative to viability.
  - APS defines life as viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
  - Agency, Process, and Scale are analytic projections of that one organisation.
  - Philosophy of biology emerges from the explanatory structure of biology itself.

FILE: src\content\glossary\autopoiesis.md
================================================================
title: Autopoiesis
slug: autopoiesis
type: glossary
definition: "A theory of life developed by Humberto Maturana and Francisco Varela according to which living systems are self-producing organisations that continuously generate and maintain the components constituting their own organisational unity."
inBrief: "Autopoiesis explains living systems as self-producing organisations that maintain themselves through their own activity."
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-17
date: 2026-06-17
revised: 2026-06-17

  Awareness does not occupy a single stage within the APSI pathway. It functions as a distributed and graded mode of sensitivity through which significance becomes available to living systems across multiple forms of organisation.

FILE: src\content\glossary\behaviour.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-03
title: Behaviour
slug: behaviour
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-03
revised: 2026-07-03
cluster: cognition-and-mind

definition: >
  Behaviour is the observable expression of biological agency through
  context-sensitive interactions with internal and external conditions.
  Within APS, behaviour consists of the actions, responses, and regulatory
  activities by which living systems engage with conditions affecting
  viability, functioning, and adaptive persistence.

inBrief: >
  Behaviour is how biological agency is expressed in action. It consists of
  the context-sensitive responses and activities through which living
  systems interact with conditions that matter for organised persistence.

================================================================
FILE: src\content\glossary\biological-individual.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-27
title: Biological Individual
slug: biological-individual
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-27
revised: 2026-07-02

cluster: conceptual-foundations

definition: >
  A biological individual is a viability-oriented system that exhibits
  sufficient organisational integration and continuity to function as a
  coherent biological entity. In APS, individuality is defined primarily
  by patterns of organised persistence rather than by fixed material
  boundaries, genetic identity, or complete independence. Biological
  individuals may occur in different forms and at different scales, and
  may exhibit varying degrees of organismality depending on the extent
  to which biological processes are integrated into a coherent,
  self-maintaining whole.

inBrief: >
  A biological individual is a viability-oriented and organisationally
  integrated system that maintains coherent continuity through change.

seeAlso:
  - organism
  - organismality
  - biological-agency
  - organised-persistence
  - organisational-continuity
  - biological-organisation
  - developmental-organisation
  - ecological-organisation
  - regulation
  - viability
  - continuity
  - constraint-closure

================================================================
FILE: src\content\glossary\biological-meaning.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-03
title: Biological Meaning
slug: biological-meaning
type: glossary
status: draft
canonical: false
canonicalLockDate:
revised: 2026-07-03
cluster: cognition-and-mind

definition: >
  Biological meaning is the significance that conditions, differences,
  events, relationships, or states possess for a living system in relation
  to viability, functioning, and adaptive persistence. Within APS,
  biological meaning emerges from the ways living systems differentially
  engage with conditions that matter for organised persistence.

inBrief: >
  Biological meaning is the significance that something has for a living
  system. It arises when conditions matter differently for viability,
  functioning, and adaptive persistence, allowing organisms to distinguish
  among opportunities, resources, threats, and constraints.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - biological-agency
  - evaluation
  - biological-value
  - cognition
  - semiosis
  - information
  - normativity
  - behaviour

relatedArticles:
  - what-is-biological-meaning

FILE: src\content\glossary\biological-value.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-03
title: Biological Value
slug: biological-value
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-03
revised: 2026-07-03
cluster: cognition-and-mind

definition: >
  A biological value is a pattern of significance that emerges from the
  evaluative activities of living systems. Biological values reflect the
  relative importance of conditions, resources, relationships, or outcomes
  for viability, functioning, and adaptive persistence, helping guide
  behaviour and regulation across biological scales.

inBrief: >
  Biological values are patterns of importance that emerge when living
  systems repeatedly evaluate conditions according to their significance
  for viability, functioning, and adaptive persistence. They help explain
  why some conditions matter more than others and guide behaviour and
  regulation throughout the living world.

title: Communication
slug: communication
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-30
revised: 2026-08-03
cluster: cognition-and-mind

definition: >
  Communication is the coordination of activity through the production,
  transmission, interpretation, and use of signals, signs, symbols, or other
  communicative behaviours. Communication is understood primarily through the coordination it achieves rather than through the transmission of information considered independently of its organisational consequences. Communication contributes to organised persistence by stabilising
  interactions, reducing uncertainty, and supporting coordinated activity
  among viability-oriented organisms across time and scale.

inBrief: >
  Communication is a mechanism of coordination through which living systems
  organise activity and sustain continuity.

FILE: src\content\glossary\conceptual-stability.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-26
title: Conceptual Stability
slug: conceptual-stability
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-26
revised: 2026-07-26
cluster: methodology-and-explanation

definition: >
  Conceptual Stability is the condition in which a concept possesses
  sufficiently clear meaning, explanatory boundaries, and relationships
  to neighbouring concepts to support consistent scientific communication,
  investigation, and explanation. Within APS, Conceptual Stability is
  achieved through naturalisation and strengthened through conceptual
  closure, allowing concepts to participate reliably in explanatory
  grammar, dependency pathways, and scientific inquiry while remaining
  open to justified refinement. Conceptual Stability therefore describes
  the operational reliability of concepts within an explanatory framework
  rather than their permanence or immunity from revision.

inBrief: >
  Conceptual Stability is the condition that enables concepts to function
  reliably within scientific explanation.

seeAlso:
  - naturalisation
  - conceptual-closure
  - scientific-intelligibility
  - conceptual-infrastructure
  - explanatory-grammar
  - dependency-pathways

FILE: src\content\glossary\coordination.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-30
title: Coordination
slug: coordination
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-30
revised: 2026-06-15
cluster: cognition-and-mind

definition: >
  Coordination is the organised alignment of activities, processes, or
  behaviours among interacting components of a system such that their
  interactions contribute to organised persistence. In APS, coordination is a
  fundamental organisational process through which living systems regulate
  relationships across time and scale. Coordination may occur within
  organisms, among organisms, or across larger social and ecological systems,
  and provides the foundation for communication, normativity, culture,
  institutions, and other forms of socially distributed continuity.

inBrief: >
  Coordination is the organised alignment of activity that contributes to
  organised persistence.

FILE: src\content\glossary\culture.md
================================================================
date: 2026-05-30
title: Culture
slug: culture
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-30
revised: 2026-06-15
cluster: social-organisation

definition: >
  Culture is a socially distributed form of organised persistence through
  which practices, meanings, expectations, skills, knowledge, and symbolic
  systems are stabilised, reproduced, and transmitted across generations.
  In APS, culture emerges from communication, social norms, and symbolic
  coordination, enabling continuity to extend beyond individual organisms
  into historically persistent forms of social organisation. Culture
  therefore functions as a continuity architecture that preserves and
  reproduces coordinated activity through time.

inBrief: >
  Culture is socially distributed organised persistence maintained across
  generations through shared practices, meanings, and symbolic systems.

FILE: src\content\glossary\diagnosis.md

date: 2026-04-13
title: Diagnosis
slug: diagnosis
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-04-13
revised: 2026-06-15
cluster: diagnostics-and-empirical-tractability
definition: >
  Diagnosis is the evaluation of a system’s organisation in terms of its capacity to sustain viability. In APS, diagnosis is not the identification of a static condition or the classification of a system into predefined categories, but the interpretation of how a system responds to perturbation relative to its own persistence. It reveals whether and how a system maintains, modulates, or loses the organisation required for continued existence.
inBrief: >
  Diagnosis is the evaluation of a system’s viability-oriented organisation through its response to perturbation.

FILE: src\content\glossary\functional-integration.md
================================================================
title: Functional Integration
slug: functional-integration
type: glossary

definition: >
  Functional Integration is the coordinated organisation of
  viability-oriented biological functions through which their combined
  activity contributes to the continued viability and organised
  persistence of a living system. Within APS, biological functions do
  not possess their full biological significance in isolation.
  Individual functions become biologically significant through their
  integration with other functions within the organisation of the living
  system. Metabolism, physiological regulation, repair, development,
  behaviour, and reproduction each contribute to viability, but none is
  sufficient independently. Functional Integration therefore explains
  how diverse biological functions are coordinated into a coherent
  organisational whole whose integrated activity maintains the
  conditions required for continued viability.

inBrief: >
  Functional Integration explains how diverse biological functions are
  coordinated into a coherent organisational whole that maintains the
  viability and organised persistence of the living system.

date: 2026-07-25
revised: 2026-07-27

  - organised-persistence

FILE: src\content\glossary\functional-organisation.md
================================================================
title: Functional Organisation
slug: functional-organisation
type: glossary

definition: >
  Functional Organisation is the coordinated arrangement and ongoing
  integration of viability-oriented biological functions through which a
  living system maintains and continually re-establishes the conditions
  of its own persistence. Within APS, biological functions do not
  operate independently. Each function derives its biological
  significance from its contribution to the wider organisation of the
  living system and from its dependence upon other functions within that
  organisation. Functional Organisation therefore describes the
  coordinated organisational activity through which diverse biological
  functions collectively realise the continuing organisation of life. It
  is not a fixed arrangement of parts but an ongoing organisational
  accomplishment continually enacted, adjusted, and maintained as living
  systems undergo material turnover, development, and environmental
  change.

inBrief: >
  Functional Organisation explains how biological functions are
  coordinated into a coherent viability-oriented whole through which
  living systems continually maintain organised persistence.

date: 2026-07-25
revised: 2026-07-27

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-25

cluster: conceptual-foundations

seeAlso:
  - biological-function
  - functional-integration
  - functional-equivalence
  - adaptive-reorganisation
  - operational-persistence
  - viability
  - organised-persistence

references: []

FILE: src\content\glossary\goal.md

date: 2026-07-02
title: Goal
slug: goal
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-02
revised: 2026-07-02

cluster: conceptual-foundations

definition: >
  A goal is a state, condition, or outcome toward which biological
  activity is directed through processes of regulation, coordination,
  or adaptive behaviour. In APS, goals emerge from biological agency
  and the maintenance of viability rather than from external design or
  necessarily conscious intention. Goals help organise biological
  activity by providing direction to processes that contribute to the
  persistence, stability, adaptation, and continuity of living systems.

inBrief: >
  A goal is a state or outcome toward which biological activity is
  directed in support of viability and organised persistence.

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-17

  In APS, information is not a fundamental property of life but an
  organisationally derived relation in which differences participate in
  viability-oriented regulation within organised persistence. Information
  depends upon evaluation, semiosis, meaning, and persistence-maintaining
  organisation rather than constituting the foundational basis of biological
  activity.

  Information becomes biologically significant only within systems already
  organised around viability-oriented persistence. Information is therefore
  derivative of evaluation and semiosis rather than foundational to life
  itself.

date: 2026-05-15
title: Mechanism
slug: mechanism
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-16
revised: 2026-07-28
cluster:  philosophy-of-biology

definition: >
  In APS, a mechanism is an organised set of processes, interactions, and
  constraints through which particular activities are generated within living
  systems. Mechanisms are biologically meaningful only within
  viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and therefore do not
  exhaust biological explanation independently of organised persistence.

inBrief: >
  Mechanisms explain how biological processes operate, but their biological
  significance depends upon viability-oriented organisation rather than
  isolated causal activity alone.

FILE: src\content\glossary\operational-persistence.md
================================================================
title: Operational Persistence
slug: operational-persistence
type: glossary

definition: >
  Operational Persistence is the continuing organisational
  accomplishment through which viability-oriented biological functions
  collectively maintain and continually re-establish the conditions
  required for the persistence of a living system. Within APS,
  Operational Persistence is not an individual biological function or
  mechanism but the ongoing achievement realised through the coordinated
  organisation of biological functions across time. It emerges through
  the integration of functional activity, the continual modulation of
  constraints, the preservation of organisational roles through
  Functional Equivalence, the ongoing adjustment of functional
  relationships through Adaptive Reorganisation, and the coordinated
  engagement of the organism with changing environmental conditions.
  Operational Persistence therefore explains how living systems actively
  maintain themselves despite continual material turnover and
  environmental change.

inBrief: >
  Operational Persistence is the continuing organisational
  accomplishment through which coordinated biological functions actively
  maintain the viability of a living system.

date: 2026-07-25
revised: 2026-07-27

status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-25

cluster: conceptual-foundations

seeAlso:
  - biological-function
  - functional-organisation
  - functional-integration
  - functional-equivalence

  - constraint-modulation
  - organised-persistence

  - viability

FILE: src\content\glossary\organismality.md
================================================================
date: 2026-07-02
title: Organismality
slug: organismality
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-02
revised: 2026-07-02
cluster: conceptual-foundations

definition: >
  Organismality refers to the degree to which a living system functions as an
  integrated organism. Rather than treating organismhood as an all-or-nothing
  category, organismality focuses on the extent to which biological processes
  are coordinated into a coherent, self-maintaining, and functionally
  integrated whole. Characteristics commonly associated with organismality
  include physiological integration, developmental coordination, functional
  interdependence, behavioural organisation, and the capacity to maintain
  viability under changing conditions. In APS, organismality is understood as
  a manifestation of biological agency and organised persistence, reflecting
  the integration of multiple biological processes into coherent patterns of
  regulation and continuity across time.

inBrief: >
  Organismality is the degree to which a living system exhibits the integrated,
  coordinated, and self-maintaining characteristics associated with organisms.

seeAlso:
  - organism
  - biological-individuality
  - biological-agency
  - organised-persistence
  - regulation
  - viability
  - continuity
  - biological-scale
  - what-is-an-organism
  - what-is-the-fundamental-unit-of-life

FILE: src\content\glossary\perturbation.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-18
title: Perturbation
slug: perturbation
type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-04-13
revised: 2026-06-15
cluster: diagnostics-and-empirical-tractability
definition: >
  Perturbation is the deliberate or incidental alteration of a system’s conditions, components, or interactions in order to probe the organisation that sustains its viability. In APS, perturbations are not merely disturbances to isolated parts but interventions into the constraint-closed organisation of a system. Their significance lies in how the system responds relative to its own viability: whether it compensates, reorganises, degrades, or collapses. Perturbation therefore serves as a primary methodological tool for revealing organisational causation, normativity, and the degree of biological agency.
inBrief: >
  Perturbation is the probing of a system’s viability-oriented organisation through targeted disturbance.
seeAlso:
  - biological-agency
  - viability
  - constraint-closure

type: glossary
status: canonical
canonical: true

cluster: social-organisation

definition: >
  Symbolic coordination is the organisation of activity through shared
  symbolic systems that stabilise meanings, expectations, and forms of
  coordination across time and scale. In APS, symbols are understood
  primarily as mechanisms of coordination rather than merely as
  representations. Symbolic coordination extends organised persistence by
  enabling continuity-preserving organisation beyond immediate interaction,
  supporting the emergence of culture, institutions, and technology.

  coordinated organised persistence.

FILE: src\content\orientation\aps-challenges-limitations-and-future-directions.md
================================================================
date: 2026-06-08
title: "APS Challenges, Limitations, and Future Directions"
slug: aps-challenges-limitations-and-future-directions
type: orientation
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-06-08
revised: 2026-08-11
cluster: conceptual-foundations

abstract: >
  APS is a developing framework and research programme in theoretical
  biology that investigates organised persistence through comparative
  organisational explanation. This article defines the present boundaries
  of APS claims, explains how its concepts and applications should be
  evaluated, and distinguishes established clarifications from partly
  resolved and open scientific objections. It identifies six principal
  challenges concerning explanatory gain, operationalisation, conceptual
  discrimination, novelty, organisational transformation, and
  transferability, and outlines the research through which APS may be
  strengthened, restricted, or revised.

keyPoints:
  - APS is a developing framework and research programme rather than a completed theory of biology.
  - Organised persistence is a biological explanatory target, while the Organised Persistence methodology provides a systematic approach to comparative organisational investigation.
  - The scientific standing of APS depends upon identifiable explanatory gain relative to specified biological questions and comparison accounts.
  - APS claims require claim-appropriate evaluation and must remain open to limited gain, redundancy, inapplicability, failure, restriction, and revision.
  - Operationalising viability and organised persistence requires domain-specific specification of systems, viability conditions, constraints, perturbations, responses, evidence, and failure boundaries.
  - APS must demonstrate that agency, constraint, function, Scale, and Architectural Dependency distinguish biologically relevant phenomena rather than merely redescribing familiar processes.
  - The novelty of APS is architectural and methodological and must be tested through direct comparison with established biological and philosophical approaches.
  - Future research should prioritise focused biological demonstrations, comparative benchmarking, boundary and negative cases, transferability, evolutionary transformation, and independent application.

relatedGlossaryTerms:
  - biological-agency
  - viability
  - organised-persistence
  - architectural-dependency
  - normativity
  - biological-explanation
  - evolution
  - development
  - cognition

date: 2026-04-29
title: APS in One Page — The Structure of Explanation
slug: aps-in-one-page-the-structure-of-explanation
type: orientation
status: canonical
revised: 2026-04-29
cluster: conceptual-foundations
abstract: >
  This article provides a concise overview of the explanatory structure of the APS framework. It presents the core sequence through which biological organisation is analysed, showing how viability-oriented activity gives rise to evaluation, semiosis, integration, temporal depth, and cognition.
keyPoints:
  - APS explanation proceeds from viability-oriented organisation.
  - Evaluation is the fundamental modulation of activity relative to persistence.
  - Semiosis structures what matters for the system.
  - Integration coordinates activity across processes, time, and scale.
  - Temporal depth allows regulation beyond immediate conditions.
  - Cognition arises as a structured development of evaluative activity.

FILE: src\content\orientation\the-core-structure-of-aps-how-the-framework-fits-together.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-13
title: "The Core Structure of APS — How the Framework Fits Together"
slug: the-core-structure-of-aps-how-the-framework-fits-together
type: orientation
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-07-10
revised: 2026-09-10
cluster: conceptual-foundations

abstract: >
  APS is an integrative analytic framework for theoretical biology. This article
  presents the conceptual architecture through which it organises inquiry into
  living organisation and organised persistence. Agency, Process, and Scale are
  complementary, target-sensitive analytic projections of one
  viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation: Agency concerns what
  living systems do, Process concerns how continuity is maintained despite
  change, and Scale concerns where persistence is organised across spatial and
  temporal extents. APS also proposes dependency hypotheses concerning
  biological evaluation, significance, integration, cognition, mind, selfhood,
  reflective agency, meaning, value, morality, and ethics. These are
  proposition-specific research commitments of differing evidential status,
  not consequences established by the coherence of the architecture itself.

keyPoints:
  - APS is an integrative analytic framework for theoretical biology whose strongest demonstrated contribution is bounded analytic and methodological organisation of explanatory inquiry.
  - Organised persistence identifies continuity of living organisation despite change as an explanandum-organising concept; general additional substantive explanatory gain has not been demonstrated.
  - Agency, Process, and Scale are complementary analytic projections of one viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation.
  - Agency concerns what living systems do; Process concerns how continuity is maintained despite change; Scale concerns where persistence is organised across spatial and temporal extents.
  - Agency, Process, and Scale form a structured analytic interface; they are not components, causes, entities, domains, levels, or universally required categories of biological reality or explanation.
  - The distinctions among Agency, Process, and Scale are analytic; the organisation and activities to which they direct attention are biologically real.
  - Life, Agency, Normativity, Function, Biological Evaluation and significance, Purpose, Cognition and the wider dependency pathways remain APS commitments, formulations or hypotheses with proposition-specific evidential status.
  - Conceptual coherence, integration and usability do not establish substantive biological truth, general explanatory superiority or foundational indispensability.

relatedGlossaryTerms:

FILE: src\content\orientation\what-is-aps-ci.md
================================================================
date: 2026-04-13
title: "What Is APS?"
slug: what-is-aps
type: orientation
status: canonical
canonical: true
canonicalLockDate: 2026-05-26
revised: 2026-09-10
cluster: conceptual-foundations
abstract: >
  APS is an integrative analytic framework for theoretical biology. It investigates
  living organisation and assesses what an organisational perspective contributes
  to biological explanation. Its strongest demonstrated contribution is the bounded
  analytic and methodological organisation of explanatory inquiry. It proposes that
  Life is viability-oriented, constraint-closed organisation and distinguishes the
  continuity of that living organisation through change as organised persistence.
  Agency, Process, and Scale provide complementary analytic projections through
  which living organisation can be investigated without treating them as components,
  levels, or independent causes.
